Oh, no, this goes way back. I mean, work has made it more obvious because there’s more on the line, but I remember being like this in grade school too.

Teachers used to say I was bright, but I was always losing papers or forgetting to turn something in, or I’d only do part of the directions because I missed the rest somehow. My desk was always a mess too—well, everybody’s desk was a little messy, but mine was the one where the permission slip would disappear and then turn up weeks later. And they were always reminding me to finish classwork, like I’d start it and then somehow not get to the end unless someone came by and nudged me. So yeah, it’s not new. It’s just catching up with me harder now.

I mean, work has made it more obvious because there’s more on the line, but I remember being like this in grade school too.\n\nTeachers used to say I was bright, but I was always losing papers or forgetting to turn something in, or I’d only do part of the directions because I missed the rest somehow. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t remember anyone making a big deal about me being out of my seat or talking too much. My brother was the loud one in the house, so if anybody got told to sit down and stop climbing on stuff, it was usually him.

I was more… I don’t know, I’d get in trouble for other things, but as far as acting before thinking, I’m sure I did dumb kid stuff. Everybody did. Like one time we tried to make a ramp for bikes out of old plywood behind the garage, and my mom still brings that up—though that might’ve just been normal kid stupidity, not anything special.

So, no, I don’t have a clear memory of teachers saying that about me. It was more like I was always being reminded or corrected, but I can’t really say it was for not staying seated or talking too much.

Yeah, I mean… it’s not just work, but work is where it’s gotten me in real trouble. Yeah, definitely. Like I’ll start a digital work order thinking, okay, I’ve got this, I’m reading the notes, I know what needs doing… and then halfway through, some noise happens or somebody walks by or I think about the next job, and suddenly I realize I don’t actually know what I just read.

But now it’s causing real problems.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you've struggled with this kind of forgetfulness and losing track of tasks for as long as you can remember, even back in school, or is this something that's really only become a problem more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, this goes way back. I mean, work has made it more obvious because there’s more on the line, but I remember being like this in grade school too.\n\nTeachers used to say I was bright, but I was always losing papers or forgetting to turn something in, or I’d only do part of the directions because I missed the rest somehow. My desk was always a mess too—well, everybody’s desk was a little messy, but mine was the one where the permission slip would disappear and then turn up weeks later. And they were always reminding me to finish classwork, like I’d start it and then somehow not get to the end unless someone came by and nudged me. So yeah, it’s not new. It’s just catching up with me harder now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or find it hard to sit still, or do you tend to act or speak impulsively—like interrupting others or blurting things out—in daily life?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really? I mean, I can get antsy if I’m sitting too long, like anybody, especially in those waiting room chairs—those things are awful, and I always wonder who picks them because nobody’s comfortable in them.\n\nBut the interrupting or blurting stuff… I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as, like, jumping out of my skin all the time. At work I’m usually moving around anyway, so it’s hard to tell what I’d be like sitting still all day. I guess I’d have to think about that more.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to when you were a kid, did teachers or family ever say you had trouble staying seated, talked excessively, or acted before thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t remember anyone making a big deal about me being out of my seat or talking too much. My brother was the loud one in the house, so if anybody got told to sit down and stop climbing on stuff, it was usually him.\n\nI was more… I don’t know, I’d get in trouble for other things, but as far as acting before thinking, I’m sure I did dumb kid stuff. Everybody did. Like one time we tried to make a ramp for bikes out of old plywood behind the garage, and my mom still brings that up—though that might’ve just been normal kid stupidity, not anything special.\n\nSo, no, I don’t have a clear memory of teachers saying that about me. It was more like I was always being reminded or corrected, but I can’t really say it was for not staying seated or talking too much.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice similar problems now—like losing track of personal tasks, misplacing things, or forgetting appointments at home or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s not just work, but work is where it’s gotten me in real trouble. What brought me in is I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks and work orders and even little follow-ups I told people I’d handle. And then they pile up, and once they’re piled up it’s like—well, you know when you have a junk drawer and you swear you’re going to clean it, but then suddenly there’s batteries and old keys and takeout menus from a place that closed five years ago? It kind of gets like that, except with actual responsibilities.\n\nAnd now work sent me here because it’s gotten bad enough that I could mess up my chance at moving up. That’s the part that scares me, honestly. I’ve worked hard, and I don’t want to lose my shot because I can’t keep track of what I said I’d do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling persistently down, anxious, or unusually stressed lately that might be affecting your concentration, or does this forgetfulness happen even when your mood feels fine?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t think it’s only when I’m upset or stressed. Work is stressful now because of it, if that makes sense, like it became stressful after I kept dropping things.\n\nWhat brought me in is I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and even basic follow-ups I told people I’d handle. And then it piles up, and once it piles up it’s like I don’t even know where to start. Work finally sent me here because they’re worried, and honestly I’m worried too, because I don’t want this to ruin my chance at moving up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're easily distracted by things going on around you—like noises, other people talking, or things happening nearby—when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I’ll start a digital work order thinking, okay, I’ve got this, I’m reading the notes, I know what needs doing… and then halfway through, some noise happens or somebody walks by or I think about the next job, and suddenly I realize I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’m looking right at it, you know? The screen is right there, and my eyes are moving, but my brain has gone off somewhere else. Then I have to go back and reread it, sometimes more than once. And it’s not even always a big distraction—sometimes it’s just a sound in the hallway or hearing part of someone else’s conversation, and I’m gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or activities—like figuring out what to do first, planning out your day, or keeping track of steps needed to finish a job—even when you're not distracted?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from being distracted, honestly. Like… if I’m trying to plan something, there’s usually something else going on anyway, or I’ve already got three things in my head.\n\nI guess I do get kind of tangled up with where to start sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s “organizing” exactly or just me making it harder than it needs to be. Like at home, I’ll look at a pile of stuff and think, okay, laundry first, no dishes first, no wait I need to move that box—and then somehow I’m standing there thinking about whether we still have that old cooler in the garage. Which is probably not what you asked.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe, but I’m not sure I can give you a clean answer on that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I do maintenance and repair stuff, and I keep forgetting callbacks I was supposed to make, work orders I said I’d handle, even little follow-ups I promised people—and then suddenly it’s not little anymore, it’s a whole pile.\n\nMy supervisor’s been on me about it, and they basically sent me here because I’m trying to move up, and if I keep dropping things like this, I’m going to lose that chance. It’s embarrassing, because I mean to do it, I really do. I’ll tell someone, “Yeah, I’ll call you back,” and then something else happens, or I go to grab a part, and—well, you know how one thing turns into another. But now it’s causing real problems.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you've struggled with this kind of forgetfulness and losing track of tasks for as long as you can remember, even back in school, or is this something that's really only become a problem more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, this goes way back. I mean, work has made it more obvious because there’s more on the line, but I remember being like this in grade school too.\n\nTeachers used to say I was bright, but I was always losing papers or forgetting to turn something in, or I’d only do part of the directions because I missed the rest somehow. My desk was always a mess too—well, everybody’s desk was a little messy, but mine was the one where the permission slip would disappear and then turn up weeks later. And they were always reminding me to finish classwork, like I’d start it and then somehow not get to the end unless someone came by and nudged me. So yeah, it’s not new. It’s just catching up with me harder now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or find it hard to sit still, or do you tend to act or speak impulsively—like interrupting others or blurting things out—in daily life?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really? I mean, I can get antsy if I’m sitting too long, like anybody, especially in those waiting room chairs—those things are awful, and I always wonder who picks them because nobody’s comfortable in them.\n\nBut the interrupting or blurting stuff… I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as, like, jumping out of my skin all the time. At work I’m usually moving around anyway, so it’s hard to tell what I’d be like sitting still all day. I guess I’d have to think about that more.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to when you were a kid, did teachers or family ever say you had trouble staying seated, talked excessively, or acted before thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t remember anyone making a big deal about me being out of my seat or talking too much. My brother was the loud one in the house, so if anybody got told to sit down and stop climbing on stuff, it was usually him.\n\nI was more… I don’t know, I’d get in trouble for other things, but as far as acting before thinking, I’m sure I did dumb kid stuff. Everybody did. Like one time we tried to make a ramp for bikes out of old plywood behind the garage, and my mom still brings that up—though that might’ve just been normal kid stupidity, not anything special.\n\nSo, no, I don’t have a clear memory of teachers saying that about me. It was more like I was always being reminded or corrected, but I can’t really say it was for not staying seated or talking too much.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice similar problems now—like losing track of personal tasks, misplacing things, or forgetting appointments at home or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s not just work, but work is where it’s gotten me in real trouble. What brought me in is I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks and work orders and even little follow-ups I told people I’d handle. And then they pile up, and once they’re piled up it’s like—well, you know when you have a junk drawer and you swear you’re going to clean it, but then suddenly there’s batteries and old keys and takeout menus from a place that closed five years ago? It kind of gets like that, except with actual responsibilities.\n\nAnd now work sent me here because it’s gotten bad enough that I could mess up my chance at moving up. That’s the part that scares me, honestly. I’ve worked hard, and I don’t want to lose my shot because I can’t keep track of what I said I’d do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling persistently down, anxious, or unusually stressed lately that might be affecting your concentration, or does this forgetfulness happen even when your mood feels fine?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t think it’s only when I’m upset or stressed. Work is stressful now because of it, if that makes sense, like it became stressful after I kept dropping things.\n\nWhat brought me in is I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks, work orders, and even basic follow-ups I told people I’d handle. And then it piles up, and once it piles up it’s like I don’t even know where to start. Work finally sent me here because they’re worried, and honestly I’m worried too, because I don’t want this to ruin my chance at moving up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're easily distracted by things going on around you—like noises, other people talking, or things happening nearby—when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I’ll start a digital work order thinking, okay, I’ve got this, I’m reading the notes, I know what needs doing… and then halfway through, some noise happens or somebody walks by or I think about the next job, and suddenly I realize I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’m looking right at it, you know? The screen is right there, and my eyes are moving, but my brain has gone off somewhere else. Then I have to go back and reread it, sometimes more than once. And it’s not even always a big distraction—sometimes it’s just a sound in the hallway or hearing part of someone else’s conversation, and I’m gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or activities—like figuring out what to do first, planning out your day, or keeping track of steps needed to finish a job—even when you're not distracted?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from being distracted, honestly. Like… if I’m trying to plan something, there’s usually something else going on anyway, or I’ve already got three things in my head.\n\nI guess I do get kind of tangled up with where to start sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s “organizing” exactly or just me making it harder than it needs to be. Like at home, I’ll look at a pile of stuff and think, okay, laundry first, no dishes first, no wait I need to move that box—and then somehow I’m standing there thinking about whether we still have that old cooler in the garage. Which is probably not what you asked.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe, but I’m not sure I can give you a clean answer on that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding, putting off, or dreading tasks that require a lot of sustained mental effort—like paperwork, detailed reports, or planning—even when you know they’re important?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it dreading exactly. I mean, paperwork is never my favorite thing, but whose is, right? My sister actually likes forms, which I’ve never understood—she’ll sit there with little folders and labels and everything.\n\nFor me, I guess if something looks like it’s going to take a lot of thinking, I might kind of circle around it before I start, but I’m not sure if that’s avoiding it or just… trying to get myself ready. Sometimes I tell myself I’ll do it after one other thing, and then the day gets away from me.\n\nBut I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed it as a pattern, if that’s what you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I do maintenance and repair stuff, and I keep forgetting callbacks I was supposed to make, work orders I said I’d handle, even little follow-ups I promised people—and then suddenly it’s not little anymore, it’s a whole pile.\n\nMy supervisor’s been on me about it, and they basically sent me here because I’m trying to move up, and if I keep dropping things like this, I’m going to lose that chance. It’s embarrassing, because I mean to do it, I really do. I’ll tell someone, “Yeah, I’ll call you back,” and then something else happens, or I go to grab a part, and—well, you know how one thing turns into another. But now it’s causing real problems.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you've struggled with this kind of forgetfulness and losing track of tasks for as long as you can remember, even back in school, or is this something that's really only become a problem more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, this goes way back. I mean, work has made it more obvious because there’s more on the line, but I remember being like this in grade school too.\n\nTeachers used to say I was bright, but I was always losing papers or forgetting to turn something in, or I’d only do part of the directions because I missed the rest somehow. My desk was always a mess too—well, everybody’s desk was a little messy, but mine was the one where the permission slip would disappear and then turn up weeks later. And they were always reminding me to finish classwork, like I’d start it and then somehow not get to the end unless someone came by and nudged me. So yeah, it’s not new. It’s just catching up with me harder now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, fidgety, or find it hard to sit still, or do you tend to act or speak impulsively—like interrupting others or blurting things out—in daily life?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really? I mean, I can get antsy if I’m sitting too long, like anybody, especially in those waiting room chairs—those things are awful, and I always wonder who picks them because nobody’s comfortable in them.\n\nBut the interrupting or blurting stuff… I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as, like, jumping out of my skin all the time. At work I’m usually moving around anyway, so it’s hard to tell what I’d be like sitting still all day. I guess I’d have to think about that more.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to when you were a kid, did teachers or family ever say you had trouble staying seated, talked excessively, or acted before thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t remember anyone making a big deal about me being out of my seat or talking too much. My brother was the loud one in the house, so if anybody got told to sit down and stop climbing on stuff, it was usually him.\n\nI was more… I don’t know, I’d get in trouble for other things, but as far as acting before thinking, I’m sure I did dumb kid stuff. Everybody did. Like one time we tried to make a ramp for bikes out of old plywood behind the garage, and my mom still brings that up—though that might’ve just been normal kid stupidity, not anything special.\n\nSo, no, I don’t have a clear memory of teachers saying that about me. It was more like I was always being reminded or corrected, but I can’t really say it was for not staying seated or talking too much.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice similar problems now—like losing track of personal tasks, misplacing things, or forgetting appointments at home or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s not just work, but work is where it’s gotten me in real trouble. What brought me in is I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks and work orders and even little follow-ups I told people I’d handle. And then they pile up, and once they’re piled up it’s like—well, you know when you have a junk drawer and you swear you’re going to clean it, but then suddenly there’s batteries and old keys and takeout menus from a place that closed five years ago? It kind of gets like that, except with actual responsibilities.\n\nAnd now work sent me here because it’s gotten bad enough that I could mess up my chance at moving up. That’s the part that scares me, honestly. I’ve worked hard, and I don’t want to lose my shot because I can’t keep track of what I said I’d do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling persistently down, anxious, or unusually stressed lately that might be affecting your concentration, or does this forgetfulness happen even when your mood feels fine?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t think it’s only when I’m upset or stressed. And then it piles up, and once it piles up it’s like I don’t even know where to start. Work finally sent me here because they’re worried, and honestly I’m worried too, because I don’t want this to ruin my chance at moving up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're easily distracted by things going on around you—like noises, other people talking, or things happening nearby—when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I’ll start a digital work order thinking, okay, I’ve got this, I’m reading the notes, I know what needs doing… and then halfway through, some noise happens or somebody walks by or I think about the next job, and suddenly I realize I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’m looking right at it, you know? The screen is right there, and my eyes are moving, but my brain has gone off somewhere else. Then I have to go back and reread it, sometimes more than once. Like… if I’m trying to plan something, there’s usually something else going on anyway, or I’ve already got three things in my head.\n\nI guess I do get kind of tangled up with where to start sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s “organizing” exactly or just me making it harder than it needs to be. Like at home, I’ll look at a pile of stuff and think, okay, laundry first, no dishes first, no wait I need to move that box—and then somehow I’m standing there thinking about whether we still have that old cooler in the garage. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I can remember that kind of thing from grade school. I don’t know if I can pull up one perfect example with the exact grade and teacher, but I remember teachers saying I was bright, like I understood things, but I was always losing papers or forgetting to turn things in.

There were times I’d do part of an assignment and miss a whole section because I didn’t catch all the directions, and then I’d get marked down or have to stay in at recess or something to finish it. And I remember needing reminders to finish classwork, like I’d just… not get it completed unless someone came back around and told me to keep going.

It’s funny, I can picture the desks and those paper folders more than the actual assignment. I was always cramming loose papers into them and then somehow the one I needed would disappear. But yeah, this was way before work stress or anything like that. It was back in school.

I mean, I’m not really sure what counts as “a lot” of caffeine. I’ll have coffee, but then some days at work somebody brings one of those huge gas station cups and everybody acts like that’s normal, so I don’t know where the line is.\n\nAlcohol… I wouldn’t say it’s something I’ve connected to my focus. Recreational drugs, no—well, I mean, not like something that’s part of my life. I haven’t really sat down and compared “this day I drank coffee” to “this day I forgot things,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nMy focus problems feel like they’re just kind of there, but I don’t know. Maybe I should pay more attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a specific instance from before you were about 12 years old—like a missed homework assignment or a forgotten school project—where this kind of forgetfulness actually caused a real consequence, like a bad grade or getting in trouble?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I can remember that kind of thing from grade school. I don’t know if I can pull up one perfect example with the exact grade and teacher, but I remember teachers saying I was bright, like I understood things, but I was always losing papers or forgetting to turn things in.\n\nThere were times I’d do part of an assignment and miss a whole section because I didn’t catch all the directions, and then I’d get marked down or have to stay in at recess or something to finish it. And I remember needing reminders to finish classwork, like I’d just… not get it completed unless someone came back around and told me to keep going.\n\nIt’s funny, I can picture the desks and those paper folders more than the actual assignment. I was always cramming loose papers into them and then somehow the one I needed would disappear. But yeah, this was way before work stress or anything like that. It was back in school."

Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.   }
]

Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah, definitely. Like I’ll start a digital work order thinking, okay, I’ve got this, I’m reading the notes, I know what needs doing… and then halfway through, some noise happens or somebody walks by or I think about the next job, and suddenly I realize I don’t actually know what I just read.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’m looking right at it, you know? The screen is right there, and my eyes are moving, but my brain has gone off somewhere else. Then I have to go back and reread it, sometimes more than once.   }

Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t know if I’d call it dreading exactly. I mean, paperwork is never my favorite thing, but whose is, right? My sister actually likes forms, which I’ve never understood—she’ll sit there with little folders and labels and everything.\n\nFor me, I guess if something looks like it’s going to take a lot of thinking, I might kind of circle around it before I start, but I’m not sure if that’s avoiding it or just… trying to get myself ready. Sometimes I tell myself I’ll do it after one other thing, and then the day gets away from me.\n\nBut I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed it as a pattern, if that’s what you mean.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.
